======================================================================
ARTICLE: Wastewater
WIKIPEDIA REVISION TEXT
======================================================================

{{Short description|Water that has been used and contaminated}}
{{Distinguish|Wast Water}}

{{Pollution sidebar}}

'''Wastewater''' is water generated after the use of [[drinking water]], [[fresh water]], [[raw water]], or [[saline water]] in a variety of deliberate applications or processes.<ref name=":1">{{Cite book|title=Wastewater engineering : treatment and reuse|date=2003|publisher=McGraw-Hill|first1=George |last1=Tchobanoglous |first2=Franklin L. |last2=Burton |first3=H. David |last3=Stensel |last4=Metcalf & Eddy|isbn=0-07-041878-0|edition=4th|location=Boston|oclc=48053912}}</ref>{{rp|1}} Another definition of wastewater is "Used water from any combination of domestic, industrial, commercial or agricultural activities, surface runoff / storm water, and any [[infiltration and inflow|sewer infiltration or sewer inflow]]".<ref name=":0">{{cite book|last1=Tilley|first1=E.|last2=Ulrich|first2=L.|last3=Lüthi|first3=C.|last4=Reymond|first4=Ph.|last5=Zurbrügg|first5=C.|url=http://www.eawag.ch/en/department/sandec/publications/compendium/|title=Compendium of Sanitation Systems and Technologies –|publisher=[[Swiss Federal Institute of Aquatic Science and Technology]] (Eawag), Duebendorf, Switzerland|year=2014|isbn=978-3-906484-57-0|edition=2nd Revised|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160408021403/http://www.eawag.ch/en/department/sandec/publications/compendium/|archive-date=8 April 2016|url-status=live|df=dmy-all}}</ref>{{rp|175}} In everyday usage, wastewater is commonly a synonym for [[sewage]] (also called domestic wastewater or municipal wastewater), which is wastewater that is produced by a community of people.

== Types==
Wastewater is a generic term that may refer to water containing contaminants originating from various settings. Major categories include:

* [[Sewage|Domestic wastewater]], which encompasses sewage produced by communities and is commonly subdivided into [[greywater]] and [[Blackwater (waste)|blackwater]].
* [[Industrial wastewater]]: waterborne waste generated from a variety of industrial processes, such as manufacturing operations, mineral extraction, power generation, or water and [[wastewater treatment]].
* [[Cooling water]], is released with potential [[thermal pollution]] after use to condense steam or reduce machinery temperatures by conduction or evaporation.
* [[Leachate]]: precipitation containing pollutants dissolved while percolating through ores, raw materials, products, or solid waste.
* [[Return flow]]: the flow of water carrying suspended soil, pesticide residues, or dissolved minerals and nutrients from irrigated [[cropland]].
* [[Surface runoff]]: the flow of water occurring on the ground surface when excess [[rain]]water, [[stormwater]], [[meltwater]], or other sources, can no longer sufficiently rapidly infiltrate the [[soil]].
* [[Urban runoff]], including water used for outdoor cleaning activity and landscape irrigation in densely populated areas created by [[urbanization]].
* [[Agricultural wastewater treatment|Agricultural wastewater]]: [[animal husbandry]] wastewater generated from [[Intensive animal farming|confined animal]] operations.

== Treatment ==
{{Main|Wastewater treatment}}
Wastewater treatment refers to processes used to remove [[Contamination|contaminants]] from wastewater. The treated [[effluent]] is typically discharged to a receiving water body with the aim of limiting adverse environmental impacts.<ref>{{Cite news |title=Wastewater treatment {{!}} Process, History, Importance, Systems, & Technologies {{!}} Britannica |url=https://www.britannica.com/technology/wastewater-treatment |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260113015504/https://www.britannica.com/technology/wastewater-treatment |archive-date=2026-01-13 |access-date=2026-01-19 |work=Encyclopedia Britannica |language=en}}</ref> Sewage is usually treated at sewage treatment plants. Industrial wastewater may be treated at facilities designed for industrial processes or, in some cases, at municipal sewage treatment plants.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Membracon |date=2022-08-14 |title=What is a Sewage Treatment Plant & How does it work? |url=https://www.membracon.co.uk/blog/what-is-a-sewage-treatment-plant-how-does-it-work-2/ |access-date=2026-01-19 |website=Membracon |language=en-US}}</ref> When the latter occurs, industries generally perform on-site pretreatment before discharge to the municipal system. Other specialized treatment plants exist for agricultural wastewater and leachate.

Treatment processes commonly include [[phase separation]], biological and chemical transformation steps, and polishing to improve effluent quality. [[Sludge]] is the principal by-product generated during treatment and is further processed at the same facility or at separate sludge treatment plants.<ref>{{Citation |last=Bustillo-Lecompte |first=Ciro |chapter=Slaughterhouse Wastewater: Treatment, Management and Resource Recovery |date=2017-05-03 |title=Physico-Chemical Wastewater Treatment and Resource Recovery |chapter-url=https://www.intechopen.com/chapters/52474 |access-date=2026-01-19 |publisher=IntechOpen |language=en |doi=10.5772/65499 |isbn=978-953-51-3130-4 |last2=Mehrvar |first2=Mehrab}}</ref> [[Aerobic treatment system|Anaerobic treatment]] processes can additionally produce [[biogas]].<ref>{{Cite book |url=http://iwaponline.com/ebooks/book/765/Anaerobic-Sewage-Treatment-Optimization-of-Process |title=Anaerobic Sewage Treatment: Optimization of Process and Physical Design of Anaerobic and Complementary Processes |date=2019-08-15 |publisher=IWA Publishing |isbn=978-1-78040-962-7 |editor-last=van Haandel |editor-first=Adrianus |edition=1 |language=en |doi=10.2166/9781780409627 |editor-last2=van der Lubbe |editor-first2=Jeroen}}</ref>

[[Category:Water supply]]

======================================================================
ARTICLE: Water conservation
WIKIPEDIA REVISION TEXT
======================================================================

{{Short description|Policies for sustainable development of water use}}
[[File:Water-conservation-stamp-1960.jpg|thumb|right|United States 1960 postal stamp advocating water conservation]]
'''Water conservation''' aims to [[sustainably]] manage the [[natural resource]] of [[fresh water]], protect the [[hydrosphere]], and meet current and future human [[demand]]. Water conservation makes it possible to avoid [[water scarcity]]. It covers all the policies, strategies and activities to reach these aims. Population, household size and growth and affluence all affect how much water is used. 

Although the terms "water efficiency" and "water conservation" are used interchangeably they are not the same. Water efficiency is the improvements such as the new technology that help with the efficiency and reduction of using water. On the other hand, water conservation is the action of conserving water. In short, water efficiency relates to the development and innovations which help use water more efficiently and water conservation is the act of saving or preserving water.<ref name=Maddaus>{{Cite journal |last1=Maddaus |first1=Lisa A. |last2=Maddaus |first2=Michelle L. |last3=Maddaus |first3=William O. |last4=Matyas |first4=Chris A. |date=2014 |title=Pursuing more efficient water use: The history and future of water conservation in the United States |url=https://www.jstor.org/stable/jamewatworass.106.8.150 |journal=Journal (American Water Works Association) |volume=106 |issue=8 |pages=150–163 |doi=10.5942/jawwa.2014.106.0115 |jstor=jamewatworass.106.8.150 |bibcode=2014JAWWA.106h.150M |issn=0003-150X|url-access=subscription }}</ref> 

[[Climate change]] and other factors have increased pressure on natural [[water resources]]. This is especially the case in [[manufacturing]] and agricultural [[irrigation]].<ref name=":0">{{cite web |title=Measures to reduce personal water use - Defra - Citizen Space |website=consult.defra.gov.uk |url=https://consult.defra.gov.uk/water/measures-to-reduce-personal-water-use/ |access-date=2021-09-13}}</ref> Many countries have successfully implemented policies to conserve water conservation.<ref>{{cite web |title=Cases in Water Conservation: How Efficiency Programs Help Water Utilities Save Water and Avoid Costs |website=EPA.gov |publisher=US Environmental Protection Agency |url=https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyNET.exe/200041CI.TXT?ZyActionD=ZyDocument&Client=EPA&Index=2000+Thru+2005&Docs=&Query=&Time=&EndTime=&SearchMethod=1&TocRestrict=n&Toc=&TocEntry=&QField=&QFieldYear=&QFieldMonth=&QFieldDay=&IntQFieldOp=0&ExtQFieldOp=0&XmlQuery=&File=D%3A%5Czyfiles%5CIndex%20Data%5C00thru05%5CTxt%5C00000005%5C200041CI.txt&User=ANONYMOUS&Password=anonymous&SortMethod=h%7C-&MaximumDocuments=1&FuzzyDegree=0&ImageQuality=r75g8/r75g8/x150y150g16/i425&Display=hpfr&DefSeekPage=x&SearchBack=ZyActionL&Back=ZyActionS&BackDesc=Results%20page&MaximumPages=1&ZyEntry=1&SeekPage=x&ZyPURL}}</ref> There are several key activities to conserve water. One is beneficial reduction in [[drying|water loss]], use and waste of resources.<ref name=":1">{{cite journal |author1=Duane D. Baumann |author2=John J. Boland |author3=John H. Sims |date=April 1984 |title=Water Conservation: The Struggle over Definition |journal=Water Resources Research |volume=20 |issue=4 |pages=428–434 |bibcode=1984WRR....20..428B |doi=10.1029/WR020i004p00428}}</ref> Another is avoiding any damage to [[water quality]]. A third is improving [[water management]] practices that reduce the use or enhance the beneficial use of water.<ref name="Vickers, Amy 2002">{{cite book |last=Vickers |first=Amy |title=Water Use and Conservation |publisher=water plow Press |year=2002 |isbn=978-1-931579-07-0 |location=Amherst, MA |page=434}}</ref><ref name=":2">{{cite journal |last1=Geerts |first1=Sam |last2=Raes |first2=Dirk |year=2009 |title=Review: Deficit irrigation as an on-farm strategy to maximize crop water productivity in dry areas |url=https://kuleuven.limo.libis.be/discovery/fulldisplay?docid=lirias1030139&context=SearchWebhook&vid=32KUL_KUL:Lirias&search_scope=lirias_profile&adaptor=SearchWebhook&tab=LIRIAS&query=any,contains,LIRIAS1030139&offset=0&lang=en |journal=Agricultural Water Management |volume=96 |issue=9 |pages=1275–1284 |bibcode=2009AgWM...96.1275G |doi=10.1016/j.agwat.2009.04.009|url-access=subscription }}</ref> 

Technology solutions exist for households, commercial and agricultural applications to reduce the use/loss of water. Water conservation programs involved in social solutions are typically initiated at the local level, by either municipal [[water industry|water utilities]] or regional governments.

==Aims in water efficiency ==
Water conservation is the act of conserving water. Although water conservation was something people were working on it became mainstream for a number of reasons. Those reasons include supply shortages, escalating costs of infrastructure, state and federal legislative mandates, public desire to be "green", and conserving water no longer equals revenue.<ref name=Maddaus/> Supply shortages of what is meant to be a renewable resource is what most people target when using water conservation techniques.<ref name=Garcia/>

==Strategies==
The strategies to improve water conservation have similar characteristics which include having any beneficial reduction in water loss use and waste of resources, avoid any damage to water quality, and improve water management practices that reduce the use or enhance the beneficial use of water.

One of the strategies in water conservation is [[rainwater harvesting]].<ref name=":12">{{cite journal |last=Kumar Kurunthachalam |first=Senthil |year=2014 |title=Water Conservation and Sustainability: An Utmost Importance. |journal=Hydrol Current Res}}</ref> Digging ponds, lakes, canals, expanding the water reservoir, and installing rain water catching ducts and filtration systems on homes are different methods of harvesting rain water. Many people in many countries keep clean containers so they can boil it and drink it, which is useful to supply water to the needy.<ref name=":12"/> Harvested and filtered rain water can be used for toilets, home gardening, lawn irrigation, and [[smallholding|small scale agriculture]].<ref name=":12"/>

Another strategy in water conservation is protecting [[groundwater]] resources. When precipitation occurs, some infiltrates the soil and goes underground.<ref name=":22">{{cite web |title=Description of the Hydrologic Cycle |website=nwrfc.noaa.gov/rfc/ |publisher=NOAA River Forecast Center |url=http://www.nwrfc.noaa.gov/info/water_cycle/hydrology.cgi}}</ref> Water in this saturation zone is called groundwater.<ref name=":22"/> [[Groundwater pollution|Contamination of groundwater]] causes the groundwater water supply to not be able to be used as a resource of fresh drinking water and the natural regeneration of contaminated groundwater can take years to replenish.<ref name=":32">{{cite web |title=Potential threats to Groundwater |website=groundwater.org |publisher=The Groundwater Foundation |url=https://groundwater.org/threats/contamination/}}</ref> Some examples of potential sources of groundwater contamination include storage tanks, [[septic tank|septic]] systems, uncontrolled [[hazardous waste]], [[landfill]]s, [[atmospheric contaminants]], chemicals, and [[road salt]]s.<ref name=":32"/> Contamination of groundwater decreases the replenishment of available freshwater so taking preventative measures by protecting groundwater resources from contamination is an important aspect of water conservation.<ref name=":12"/>

An additional strategy to water conservation is practicing sustainable methods of utilizing groundwater resources.<ref name=":12"/> Groundwater flows due to gravity and eventually discharges into streams.<ref name=":22"/> Excess pumping of groundwater leads to a decrease in groundwater levels and if continued it can exhaust the resource.<ref name=":12"/> Ground and surface waters are connected and overuse of groundwater can reduce and, in extreme examples, diminish the water supply of lakes, rivers, and streams.<ref name=":32"/> In coastal regions, over pumping groundwater can increase [[saltwater intrusion]] which results in the contamination of groundwater water supply.<ref name=":32"/> Sustainable use of groundwater is essential in water conservation.

A fundamental component to water conservation strategy is communication and education outreach of different water programs.<ref name=":02">{{cite journal |last1=Delgado |first1=J. A. |last2=Groffman |first2=P. M. |last3=Nearing |first3=M. A. |last4=Goddard |first4=T. |last5=Reicosky |first5=D. |last6=Lal |first6=R. |last7=Kitchen |first7=N. R. |last8=Rice |first8=C. W. |last9=Towery |first9=D. |last10=Salon |first10=P. |title=Conservation practices to mitigate and adapt to climate change |journal=Journal of Soil and Water Conservation |date=1 July 2011 |volume=66 |issue=4 |pages=118A–129A |doi=10.2489/jswc.66.4.118A |doi-access=free}}</ref> Developing communication that educates science to land managers, policy makers, farmers, and the general public is another important strategy utilized in water conservation.<ref name=":02"/> Communication of the science of how water systems work is an important aspect when creating a management plan to conserve that system and is often used for ensuring the right management plan to be put into action.<ref name=":02"/>

The conservation of water is extremely important in order to preserve wildlife habitats. There are many organisms in temperate regions who are affected by shortages in water.<ref name=":5">{{cite web |date=2020-06-27 |title=Water Conservation Is an Essential Part of Wildlife Conservation |website=Green Clean Guide |language=en-US |url=https://greencleanguide.com/water-conservation-is-an-essential-part-of-wildlife-conservation/ |access-date=2022-05-30}}</ref> Additionally, many freshwater organisms are increasingly feeling the impacts of water pollution as it disrupts the ecosystem.<ref name=":5"/>

"[[World Water Day]]" is celebrated on 22 March.<ref>{{cite web |author=United Nations |title=World Water Day |website=United Nations |language=en |url=https://www.un.org/en/observances/water-day |access-date=2022-05-30}}</ref>

==Social solutions==
[[File:VineyardDrip.JPG|thumb|upright|[[Drip irrigation]] system in [[New Mexico]]]]
Water conservation programs involved in social solutions are typically initiated at the local level, by either municipal [[water industry|water utilities]] or regional governments. Common strategies include public [[outreach]] campaigns,<ref name=":3">[http://www.wateruseitwisely.com "Water - Use It Wisely."] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210719200941/https://wateruseitwisely.com/ |date=2021-07-19 }} U.S. multi-city public outreach program. Park & Co., Phoenix, AZ. Accessed 2010-02-02.</ref> tiered water rates (charging progressively higher prices as water use increases), or restrictions on outdoor water use such as lawn watering and car washing.<ref name=":4">{{cite report |title=Cases in Water Conservation {{!}} Document No. EPA-832-B-02-003 |author=U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) |year=2002 |url=http://www.epa.gov/watersense/docs/utilityconservation_508.pdf |access-date=2010-02-02 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20170107094040/https://www3.epa.gov/watersense/docs/utilityconservation_508.pdf |archive-date=2017-01-07}}</ref> Cities in dry climates often require or encourage the installation of [[xeriscaping]] or [[natural landscaping]] in new homes to reduce outdoor water usage.<ref>{{cite web |title=Xeriscape Rebates |author=Albuquerque Bernalillo County Water Utility Authority |date=2009-02-06 |location=Albuquerque, NM |url=http://www.abcwua.org/content/view/132/222/ |access-date=2010-02-02}}</ref> Most urban outdoor water use in California is residential,<ref>{{cite journal |last=Heberger |first=Matthew |year=2014 |title=Issue Brief |journal=Urban Water Conservation and Efficiency Potential in California |pages=12 |url=http://pacinst.org/app/uploads/2014/06/ca-water-urban.pdf}}</ref> illustrating a reason for outreach to households as well as businesses.

One fundamental conservation goal is universal [[water metering]]. The prevalence of residential water metering varies significantly worldwide. Recent studies have estimated that water supplies are metered in less than 30% of UK households.<ref>[http://www.innovations-report.com/html/reports/environment_sciences/report-60279.html "Time for universal water metering?"] ''Innovations Report.'' May 2006.</ref> Although individual water meters have often been considered impractical in homes with private wells or in multifamily buildings, the US [[United States Environmental Protection Agency|Environmental Protection Agency]] estimates that metering alone can reduce consumption by 20 to 40 percent.<ref name="EPA-Conserve">{{cite web |title=How to Conserve Water and Use It Effectively |author=EPA |date=2010-01-13 |location=Washington, DC |url=http://www.epa.gov/owow/nps/nps-conserve.html |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20080116153329/http://www.epa.gov/owow/nps/nps-conserve.html |url-status=dead |archive-date=January 16, 2008 |access-date=2010-02-03}}</ref> In addition to raising consumer awareness of their water use, metering is also an important way to identify and localize [[water leakage]]. Water metering might benefit society by providing a financial incentive to avoid waste in water use.<ref name="RudlinFalk2010">{{cite book |author1=David Rudlin |author2=Nicholas Falk |date=2010 |title=Sustainable Urban Neighbourhood |publisher=Routledge |page=93 |isbn=978-1-136-43490-7 |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=wswsBgAAQBAJ |quote=The first steps have included the introduction of water metering to give users a financial incentive to save water.}}</ref>

Some researchers have suggested that water conservation efforts should be primarily directed at farmers, in light of the fact that crop irrigation accounts for 70% of the world's fresh water use.<ref>{{cite journal |author=Pimentel, Berger |title=Water resources: agricultural and environmental issues |journal=BioScience |volume=54 |issue=10 |pages=909 |date=October 2004 |display-authors=etal |doi=10.1641/0006-3568(2004)054[0909:WRAAEI]2.0.CO;2 |doi-access=free}}</ref> The agricultural sector of most countries is important both economically and politically, and water subsidies are common. Conservation advocates have urged removal of all subsidies to force [[farmer]]s to grow more water-efficient crops and adopt less wasteful irrigation techniques.<ref name="Hart2013">{{cite book |author=Craig A. Hart |date=24 July 2013 |title=Climate Change and the Private Sector: Scaling Up Private Sector Response to Climate Change |publisher=Routledge |page=28 |isbn=978-1-135-01165-9 |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=9ZUqAAAAQBAJ}}</ref>

New technology poses a few new options for consumers, features such as full flush and half flush when using a [[flush toilet|toilet]] are trying to make a difference in water consumption and waste. It is also possible to use/"pollute" the water in stages (keeping use in flush toilets for last), hereby allowing more use of the water for various tasks within a same cycle (before it needs to be purified again, which can also be done in-situ). [[Earthship#Water|Earthship]]s often use such a setup.

Also available are modern [[shower head]]s that help reduce wasting water: Old shower heads are said to use 5-10 gallons per minute, while new fixtures available use 2.5 gallons per minute and offer equal water coverage.<ref>{{cite web |title=Reduce Hot Water Use for Energy Savings |website=Energy.gov |language=en |url=https://www.energy.gov/energysaver/water-heating/reduce-hot-water-use-energy-savings |access-date=2019-03-20}}</ref>
Another method is to recycle the water of the shower directly, by means a semi-closed system which features a pump and filter. Such a setup (called a "[[water recycling shower]]") has also been employed at the VIRTUe LINQ house. Besides recycling water, it also reuses the heat of the water (which would otherwise be lost).<ref>{{Cite web |url=https://www.tue.nl/nieuws/nieuwsoverzicht/team-virtue-bouwt-slim-en-duurzaam-huis-dat-mens-en-technologie-verbindt/ |title=Team VIRTUe bouwt slim en duurzaam huis dat mens en technologie verbindt |access-date=2019-05-13 |archive-date=2020-08-08 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20200808183531/https://www.tue.nl/nieuws/nieuwsoverzicht/team-virtue-bouwt-slim-en-duurzaam-huis-dat-mens-en-technologie-verbindt/ |url-status=dead }}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |url=https://teamvirtue.nl/linq/ |title=Team VIRTUe presenting LINQ |access-date=2019-05-13 |archive-date=2021-01-12 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210112022846/https://teamvirtue.nl/linq/ |url-status=dead }}</ref>

Contrary to the popular view that the most effective way to save water is to curtail water-using behavior (e.g., by taking shorter showers),<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Attari |first1=S. Z. |title=Perceptions of water use |journal=Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences |date=8 April 2014 |volume=111 |issue=14 |pages=5129–5134 |bibcode=2014PNAS..111.5129A |doi=10.1073/pnas.1316402111 |doi-access=free |pmc=3986180 |pmid=24591608}}</ref> experts suggest the most efficient way is replacing toilets and retrofitting washers; as demonstrated by two [[residential water use in the U.S. and Canada|household end use logging]] studies in the US.<ref>{{cite book |last1=Mayer |first1=Peter W. |last2=DeOreo |first2=William B. |title=Residential End Uses of Water |date=1999 |publisher=AWWA Research Foundation and American Water Works Association |isbn=978-1-58321-016-1 |url=https://www.waterdm.com/sites/default/files/WRF%20(1999)%20Residential%20End%20Uses%20of%20Water.pdf |access-date=2020-10-20 |archive-date=2022-03-24 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220324062349/https://www.waterdm.com/sites/default/files/WRF%20%281999%29%20Residential%20End%20Uses%20of%20Water.pdf |url-status=dead }}{{page needed|date=October 2020}}</ref><ref name="REUW2">{{cite book |last1=DeOreo |first1=William B. |title=Residential End Uses of Water, Version 2 |date=2016 |publisher=Water Research Foundation |isbn=978-1-60573-235-0}}{{page needed|date=October 2020}}</ref>

Some water-saving technology utilized in homes include energy-efficient water heads, composting toilets, dual flush toilets, faucet aerators, rainwater harvesting, weather-based irrigation controllers, garden hose nozzles, and automatic faucets.

Smart water meters are also a promising technology for reducing household water usage. A study conducted in Valencia, Spain, shows the potential that smart meter-based water consumption feedback has for conserving water in households. The findings showed that households that were equipped with smart water meters increased their water savings. This technology works to show people how much water they were using in their household, suggest ways they can reduce water usage, and incentivize water savings with physical rewards.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Cominola |first1=Andrea |last2=Giuliani |first2=Matteo |last3=Castelletti |first3=Andrea |last4=Fraternali |first4=Piero |last5=Gonzalez |first5=Sergio Luis Herrera |last6=Herrero |first6=Joan Carles Guardiola |last7=Novak |first7=Jasminko |last8=Rizzoli |first8=Andrea Emilio |date=2021-05-07 |title=Long-term water conservation is fostered by smart meter-based feedback and digital user engagement |journal=npj Clean Water |language=en |volume=4 |issue=1 |page=29 |issn=2059-7037 |bibcode=2021npjCW...4...29C |doi=10.1038/s41545-021-00119-0 |doi-access=free |hdl=11311/1204713 |hdl-access=free |s2cid=233876107}}</ref>

=== Applications===
Many water-saving devices (such as low-flush toilets) that are useful in homes can also be useful for business water saving. Other water-saving technology for businesses includes waterless urinals, waterless car washes, foot-operated taps, pressurized water brooms, cooling towers, water-saving steam sterilizers, rainwater harvesting, fog harvesting, and water-to-water heat exchangers. Infrared or foot-operated taps can save water by using short bursts of water for rinsing in a kitchen or bathroom. Apart from businesses, water-saving steam sterilizers can be used in hospitals and health care facilities.

A technique that is not an innovation but is helpful in preserving water is exterior messages that lead one to save water. For example, in a study conducted in 2014 showed that when an individual is exposed to a message regarding saving water they are inclined to save water.<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Richetin |first1=Juliette |last2=Perugini |first2=Marco |last3=Mondini |first3=Denny |last4=Hurling |first4=Robert |date=February 2016 |title=Conserving Water While Washing Hands: The Immediate and Durable Impacts of Descriptive Norms |url=https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0013916514543683 |journal=Environment and Behavior |language=en |volume=48 |issue=2 |pages=343–364 |doi=10.1177/0013916514543683 |bibcode=2016EnvBe..48..343R |issn=0013-9165|url-access=subscription }}</ref> As the outcome of a survey, done in 2016, that asked people how likely they are to conserve water it was found that education in the topic played a big part in the person's decision.<ref name=lgc>{{Cite journal |last1=Garcia-Cuerva |first1=Laura |last2=Berglund |first2=Emily Z. |last3=Binder |first3=Andrew R. |date=October 2016 |title=Public perceptions of water shortages, conservation behaviors, and support for water reuse in the U.S. |journal=Resources, Conservation and Recycling |language=en |volume=113 |pages=106–115 |doi=10.1016/j.resconrec.2016.06.006|bibcode=2016RCR...113..106G |doi-access=free }}</ref>

It is important to consider implementing water-conserving changes to industrial and commercial application use. It was found that high-income countries use roughly 59% of their water for industrial usage while low-income countries use 8% for industrial usage.<ref>{{cite web |title=Industrial Water {{!}} Other Uses of Water {{!}} Healthy Water |website=CDC |date=2018-10-26 |language=en-us |url=https://www.cdc.gov/healthywater/other/industrial/index.html |access-date=2022-05-30}}</ref> One big change that industrial and commercial companies can implement are to improve the assessment and maintenance of water systems.<ref name=":6">{{cite web |title=6 Steps to More Effective Water Conservation for Businesses |website=PG&E |url=https://www.pge.com/en/mybusiness/save/smbblog/article/6-steps-to-more-effective-water-conservation-for-businesses.page?redirect=yes |access-date=2022-05-30}}</ref> It is easy to add water-efficient applications but it is the proper maintenance and inspection of it which will lead to long-term changes. A water conservation plan can be created, including adding various goals and benchmarks for both the employees and the company.<ref name=":6"/> Another change that industrial and commercial companies can make are to check water-consuming systems at regular intervals for any leaks or problems.<ref name=":6"/> By doing this, it will ensure that water is not unnecessarily being lost and there is no excess money being spent on utility bills. A third change that industrial and commercial companies can implement is installing a [[rain sensor]]. This sensor should be able to detect when [[precipitation]] is occurring and stop the program which would normally [[irrigation|irrigate]] the land. After the rain ends, the sensor should turn the program back on and resume to its normal watering cycle.<ref>{{cite web |title=10 ways to save water in commercial buildings |date=2012-03-16 |website=Consulting - Specifying Engineer |language=en-US |url=https://www.csemag.com/articles/10-ways-to-save-water-in-commercial-buildings/ |access-date=2022-05-30}}</ref>

===Agricultural applications===
[[File:PivotIrrigationOnCotton.jpg|thumb|216px|right|Overhead irrigation, [[center pivot irrigation|center-pivot]] design]]
Water is an essential part of irrigation. Plants always take a lot of ground water thus ground water should be replenished. There are two types of water in agriculture which are blue and green water. Blue water is the liquid water above and below the ground while green water is the portion of water that is directly used and evaporated by rainfed agriculture.<ref name=Mancosu>{{Cite journal |last1=Mancosu |first1=Noemi |last2=Snyder |first2=Richard |last3=Kyriakakis |first3=Gavriil |last4=Spano |first4=Donatella |date=2015-03-10 |title=Water Scarcity and Future Challenges for Food Production |journal=Water |language=en |volume=7 |issue=3 |pages=975–992 |doi=10.3390/w7030975 |doi-access=free |bibcode=2015Water...7..975M |issn=2073-4441}}</ref> Although both of them are viewed as renewable only the blue water is assessed.<ref name=Mancosu/>

For crop irrigation, optimal [[water efficiency]] means minimizing losses due to [[evaporation]], [[surface runoff|runoff]], or subsurface drainage while maximizing production.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Weerasooriya |first1=R. R. |last2=Liyanage |first2=L. P. K. |last3=Rathnappriya |first3=R. H. K. |last4=Bandara |first4=W. B. M. A. C. |last5=Perera |first5=T. A. N. T. |last6=Gunarathna |first6=M. H. J. P. |last7=Jayasinghe |first7=G. Y. |date=2021-01-09 |title=Industrial water conservation by water footprint and sustainable development goals: a review |journal=Environment, Development and Sustainability |volume=23 |issue=9 |pages=12661–12709 |bibcode=2021EDSus..2312661W |issn=1387-585X |doi=10.1007/s10668-020-01184-0 |s2cid=231674040 |url=http://dx.doi.org/10.1007/s10668-020-01184-0|url-access=subscription }}</ref> An [[evaporation pan]] in combination with specific crop correction factors can be used to determine how much water is needed to satisfy plant requirements. [[Surface irrigation|Flood irrigation]], the oldest and most common type, is often very uneven in distribution, as parts of a field may receive excess water in order to deliver sufficient quantities to other parts. [[Irrigation#Overhead (sprinkler) irrigation|Overhead irrigation]], using center-pivot or lateral-moving sprinklers, has the potential for a much more equal and controlled distribution pattern. [[Drip irrigation]] is the most expensive and least-used type, but offers the ability to deliver water to plant roots with minimal losses. However, drip irrigation is increasingly affordable, especially for the home gardener and in light of rising water rates. Using drip irrigation methods can save up to 30,000 gallons of water per year when replacing irrigation systems that spray in all directions.<ref>{{cite web |title=Water-Saving Technologies |website=WaterSense: An EPA Partnership Program |publisher=US Environmental Protection Agency |url=https://www3.epa.gov/watersense/outdoor/tech.html |access-date=2016-11-17 |archive-date=2017-02-14 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20170214185923/https://www3.epa.gov/watersense/outdoor/tech.html |url-status=dead }}</ref> There are also cheap effective methods similar to drip irrigation such as the use of soaking hoses that can even be submerged in the growing medium to eliminate evaporation.

As changing irrigation systems can be a costly undertaking, conservation efforts often concentrate on maximizing the efficiency of the existing system. This may include chiselling compacted soils, creating furrow dikes to prevent runoff, and using soil moisture and rainfall sensors to optimize irrigation schedules.<ref name="EPA-Conserve"/> Usually large gains in efficiency are possible through measurement and more effective management of the existing irrigation system. The 2011 UNEP Green Economy Report notes that "[i]mproved [[soil organic matter]] from the use of green manures, mulching, and recycling of crop residues and animal manure increases the water holding capacity of soils and their ability to absorb water during torrential rains",<ref>UNEP, 2011, Towards a Green Economy: Pathways to Sustainable Development and Poverty Eradication, www.unep.org/greeneconomy</ref> which is a way to optimize the use of rainfall and irrigation during dry periods in the season.

As seen in China, plastic mulch also has the potential to conserve water in agricultural practices. The "mulch" is really a thin sheet of plastic that is placed over the soil. There are holes in the plastic for the plants to grow through. Some studies have shown that plastic mulch conserves water by reducing the evaporation of soil moisture, however, there have not been enough applied studies to determine the total water savings that this practice may bring about.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Ingman |first1=Mark |last2=Santelmann |first2=Mary V. |last3=Tilt |first3=Bryan |date=2015-06-01 |title=Agricultural water conservation in china: plastic mulch and traditional irrigation |journal=Ecosystem Health and Sustainability |volume=1 |issue=4 |pages=1–11 |issn=2096-4129 |doi=10.1890/EHS14-0018.1 |doi-access=free |bibcode=2015EcoHS...1....1I |s2cid=129853116}}</ref>

===Water reuse===
Water shortage has become an increasingly difficult problem to manage. More than 40% of the world's population live in a region where the demand for water exceeds its supply. The imbalance between supply and demand, along with persisting issues such as climate change and [[population growth]], has made [[water reuse]] a necessary method for conserving water.<ref>{{cite book |title=Wastewater Reuse and Current Challenges - Springer |volume=44 |series=The Handbook of Environmental Chemistry |year=2016 |isbn=978-3-319-23891-3 |last1=Fatta-Kassinos |first1=Despo |last2=Dionysiou |first2=Dionysios D. |last3=Kümmerer |first3=Klaus |doi=10.1007/978-3-319-23892-0 |s2cid=131884831}}</ref> There are a variety of methods used in the [[Water treatment|treatment of waste water]] to ensure that it is safe to use for irrigation of food crops and/or drinking water.

Seawater [[desalination]] requires more energy than the desalination of fresh water. Despite this, many seawater desalination plants have been built in response to water shortages around the world. This makes it necessary to evaluate the impacts of seawater desalination and to find ways to improve desalination technology. Current research involves the use of experiments to determine the most effective and least energy intensive methods of desalination.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Ibrahim |first1=Yazan |last2=Arafat |first2=Hassan A. |last3=Mezher |first3=Toufic |last4=AlMarzooqi |first4=Faisal |title=An integrated framework for sustainability assessment of seawater desalination |journal=Desalination |date=1 December 2018 |volume=447 |pages=1–17 |doi=10.1016/j.desal.2018.08.019 |bibcode=2018Desal.447....1I |s2cid=105933067}}</ref><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Elimelech |first1=M. |last2=Phillip |first2=W. A. |title=The Future of Seawater Desalination: Energy, Technology, and the Environment |journal=Science |date=5 August 2011 |volume=333 |issue=6043 |pages=712–717 |bibcode=2011Sci...333..712E |doi=10.1126/science.1200488 |pmid=21817042 |s2cid=24189246}}</ref><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Han |first1=Songlee |last2=Rhee |first2=Young-Woo |last3=Kang |first3=Seong-Pil |title=Investigation of salt removal using cyclopentane hydrate formation and washing treatment for seawater desalination |journal=Desalination |date=February 2017 |volume=404 |pages=132–137 |doi=10.1016/j.desal.2016.11.016|bibcode=2017Desal.404..132H }}</ref>

[[Sand filter|Sand filtration]] is another method used to treat water. Recent studies show that sand filtration needs further improvements, but it is approaching optimization with its effectiveness at removing pathogens from water.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Seeger |first1=Eva M. |last2=Braeckevelt |first2=Mareike |last3=Reiche |first3=Nils |last4=Müller |first4=Jochen A. |last5=Kästner |first5=Matthias |title=Removal of pathogen indicators from secondary effluent using slow sand filtration: Optimization approaches |journal=Ecological Engineering |date=October 2016 |volume=95 |pages=635–644 |doi=10.1016/j.ecoleng.2016.06.068|bibcode=2016EcEng..95..635S }}</ref><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Vries |first1=D. |last2=Bertelkamp |first2=C. |last3=Schoonenberg Kegel |first3=F. |last4=Hofs |first4=B. |last5=Dusseldorp |first5=J. |last6=Bruins |first6=J.H. |last7=de Vet |first7=W. |last8=van den Akker |first8=B. |title=Iron and manganese removal: Recent advances in modelling treatment efficiency by rapid sand filtration |journal=Water Research |date=February 2017 |volume=109 |pages=35–45 |bibcode=2017WatRe.109...35V |doi=10.1016/j.watres.2016.11.032 |pmid=27865171}}</ref> Sand filtration is very effective at removing protozoa and bacteria, but struggles with removing viruses.<ref>{{cite web |title=Slow Sand Filtration |date=May 2, 2014 |website=CDC |url=https://www.cdc.gov/safewater/sand-filtration.html}}</ref> Large-scale sand filtration facilities also require large surface areas to accommodate them.

The removal of [[pathogen]]s from recycled water is of high priority because wastewater always contains pathogens capable of infecting humans. The levels of pathogenic viruses have to be reduced to a certain level in order for recycled water to not pose a threat to human populations. Further research is necessary to determine more accurate methods of assessing the level of pathogenic viruses in treated wastewater.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Gerba |first1=Charles P. |last2=Betancourt |first2=Walter Q. |last3=Kitajima |first3=Masaaki |title=How much reduction of virus is needed for recycled water: A continuous changing need for assessment? |journal=Water Research |date=January 2017 |volume=108 |pages=25–31 |bibcode=2017WatRe.108...25G |doi=10.1016/j.watres.2016.11.020 |doi-access=free |pmc=7112101 |pmid=27838026}}</ref>

==Problems==
===Public perception of water reuse===
Answers to a survey of public perception of water shortages, conducted in 2014, showed that people were not likely to use recycled water for consumption due to fear.<ref name=lgc/> However, those who are more educated on water conservation chose the "likely" option to using recycled water.<ref name=Garcia>{{Cite journal |last1=Garcia-Cuerva |first1=Laura |last2=Berglund |first2=Emily Z. |last3=Binder |first3=Andrew R. |date=October 2016 |title=Public perceptions of water shortages, conservation behaviors, and support for water reuse in the U.S. |journal=Resources, Conservation and Recycling |language=en |volume=113 |pages=106–115 |doi=10.1016/j.resconrec.2016.06.006|bibcode=2016RCR...113..106G |doi-access=free }}</ref> Another survey, undertaken in 2021, found that some people "chose not to choose" due to fear of infection or sickness.<ref name=Li>{{Cite journal |last1=Li |first1=Tongzhe |last2=Roy |first2=Danielle |date=June 2021 |title="Choosing not to choose": Preferences for various uses of recycled water |journal=Ecological Economics |language=en |volume=184 |article-number=106992 |doi=10.1016/j.ecolecon.2021.106992|bibcode=2021EcoEc.18406992L |doi-access=free }}</ref> Researchers believe people went for the neutral option because they still wanted to choose an "environmentally friendly" option.<ref name=Li/>

===Wasting of water===
[[File:Leaking garden hose bib.jpg|thumb|upright|Leaking garden hose bib]]
Wasting water is the flip side of water conservation, and in household applications, it means causing or permitting water discharge without any practical purpose. Inefficient water use is also considered wasteful. According to [[EPA]] estimate, household leaks in the US can waste approximately 900 billion gallons (3.4 billion cubic meters) of water annually nationwide.<ref>{{cite web |title=Statistics and Facts &#124; WaterSense &#124; US EPA |website=Epa.gov |date=2017-01-23 |url=https://www.epa.gov/watersense/statistics-and-facts |access-date=2017-07-11}}</ref> Generally, water management agencies are reluctant or unwilling to give a concrete definition to a relatively vague concept of '''water waste'''.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Neuman |first1=Janet C. |title=Beneficial Use, Waste, and Forfeiture: the Inefficient Search for Efficiency in Western Water Use |journal=Environmental Law |date=1998 |volume=28 |issue=4 |pages=919–996 |jstor=43266687 |ssrn=962710 |url=http://www.nationalaglawcenter.org/wp-content/uploads/assets/bibarticles/neuman_forfeiture.pdf}}</ref>

However, definition of water waste is often given in local drought emergency ordinances. One example refers to any acts or omissions, whether willful or negligent, that are "causing or permitting water to leak, discharge, flow or run to waste into any gutter, sanitary sewer, watercourse or public or private storm drain, or to any adjacent property, from any tap, hose, faucet, pipe, sprinkler, pond, pool, waterway, fountain or nozzle."<ref>{{cite web |title=14.09.030 Definition of water waste |website=Qcode.us |url=http://qcode.us/codes/roseville/view.php?topic=14-14_09-14_09_030&frames=off |access-date=2017-07-11}}</ref> In this example, the city code also clarifies that "in the case of washing, "discharge," "flow" or "run to waste" means that water in excess of that necessary to wash, wet or clean the dirty or dusty object, such as an automobile, sidewalk, or parking area, flows to waste.

Water utilities (and other media sources) often provide listings of wasteful water-use practices and prohibitions of wasteful uses. Examples include utilities in San Antonio, Texas,<ref>{{cite web |title=SAWS Report Water Waste - What is Water Waste? |website=Saws.org |url=http://www.saws.org/conservation/waterwaste/whatiswaste.cfm |access-date=2017-07-11 |archive-date=2019-07-15 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20190715133742/http://www.saws.org/Conservation/WaterWaste/whatiswaste.cfm |url-status=dead }}</ref> Las Vegas, Nevada,<ref>{{cite web |title=Water Waste |website=Las Vegas Valley Water District |url=https://www.lvvwd.com/conservation/water-waste/index.html}}</ref> California Water Service company in California,<ref>{{cite web |title=Report Water Waste |publisher=Cal Water |date=2015-12-03 |url=https://www.calwater.com/conservation/water-waste-report/ |access-date=2017-07-11}}</ref> and City of San Diego, California.<ref>{{cite web |title=Water Saving Tips &#124; City of San Diego Official Website |website=Sandiego.gov |url=https://www.sandiego.gov/water/conservation/kids/tips |access-date=2017-07-11}}</ref> The City of Palo Alto in California enforces permanent water use restrictions on wasteful practices such as leaks, runoff, irrigating during and immediately after rainfall, and use of potable water when non-potable water is available.<ref>{{cite web |title=Water & Drought Update - Palo Alto Water Use Guidelines |url=http://www.cityofpaloalto.org/news/displaynews.asp?NewsID=2336&TargetID=235,310 |access-date=2017-08-06}}</ref> Similar restrictions are in effect in the State of Victoria, Australia.<ref>{{cite web |title=Permanent water saving rules |date=17 February 2019 |website=Victoria State Government |url=https://www.water.vic.gov.au/liveable-cities-and-towns/permanent-water-saving-rules |access-date=16 January 2018 |archive-date=14 March 2018 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20180314202819/https://www.water.vic.gov.au/liveable-cities-and-towns/permanent-water-saving-rules |url-status=dead }}</ref> Temporary water use bans (also known as "hosepipe bans") are used in England, Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.<ref>Water UK http://www.water.org.uk/consumers/tubs {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20180730213952/https://www.water.org.uk/consumers/tubs |date=2018-07-30 }}</ref>

Strictly speaking, water that is discharged into the sewer, or directly to the environment is not wasted or lost. It remains within the [[hydrologic cycle]] and returns to the land surface and surface water bodies as precipitation. However, in many cases, the source of the water is at a significant distance from the return point and may be in a different catchment. The separation between extraction point and return point can represent significant [[environmental degradation]] in the watercourse and [[Riparian zone|riparian strip]]. What is "wasted" is the community's supply of water that was captured, stored, transported and treated to drinking quality standards. Efficient use of water saves the expense of water supply provision and leaves more fresh water in lakes, rivers and [[aquifer]]s for other users and also for supporting ecosystems. For example, we should not treat toilet as a trash can. If we flush cigarette butts or tissues in it, we are wasting gallons of water. Because the process of recycling water cannot be accomplished.<ref>{{cite web |title=45+ Ways to Conserve Water in the Home and Yard |website=Eartheasy Guides & Articles |date=11 December 2017 |language=en-CA |url=https://learn.eartheasy.com/guides/45-ways-to-conserve-water-in-the-home-and-yard/ |access-date=2022-08-05}}</ref>

A concept that is closely related to water wasting is "water-use efficiency". Water use is considered [[inefficiency|inefficient]] if the same purpose of its use can be accomplished with less water. Technical [[efficiency]] derives from engineering practice where it is typically used to describe the ratio of output to input and is useful in comparing various products and processes.<ref>{{cite journal |author1=Dziegielewski, B. J. |author2=Kiefer, C. |title=Water Conservation Measurement Metrics: Guidance Report |journal=American Water Works Association |date=January 22, 2010 |url=https://www.awwa.org/Portals/0/files/resources/resource%20dev%20groups/tech%20and%20educ%20program/documents/WaterConservationMeasurementMetricsGuidanceReport.pdf |access-date=January 16, 2018 |archive-date=July 23, 2018 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20180723182149/https://www.awwa.org/Portals/0/files/resources/resource%20dev%20groups/tech%20and%20educ%20program/documents/WaterConservationMeasurementMetricsGuidanceReport.pdf |url-status=dead }}</ref> For example, one showerhead would be considered more efficient than another if it could accomplish the same purpose (i.e., of showering) by using less water or other inputs (e.g., lower water pressure). The technical efficiency concept is not useful in making decisions of investing money (or resources) in water conservation measures unless the inputs and outputs are measured in value terms. This expression of efficiency is referred to as [[economic efficiency]] and is incorporated into the concept of water conservation.

======================================================================
ARTICLE: Water pollution
WIKIPEDIA REVISION TEXT
======================================================================

{{short description|Contamination of water bodies}}
{{Redirect|Clean water|water that is safe to drink|Drinking water}}
{{protection padlock|small=yes}}
{{Use mdy dates|date=January 2012}}
[[File:Garbage floating around in Circular Quay.jpg|thumb|[[Garbage]] floating in water, [[Circular Quay]], [[Sydney]], [[Australia]]]]
{{Pollution sidebar|Water}}
'''Water pollution''' (or '''aquatic pollution''') is the [[contamination]] of [[Body of water|water bodies]], which has a negative impact on how they can be used.<ref name="Von Sperling"/>{{rp|6}} It is usually caused by human activities. Water bodies include [[lake]]s, [[river]]s, [[ocean]]s, [[aquifer]]s, [[reservoir]]s and [[groundwater]]. Water [[pollution]] results when [[contaminant]]s mix with these water bodies. [[Contamination|Contaminants]] can come from one of four main sources. These are [[sewage]] discharges, industrial activities, agricultural activities, and urban runoff including [[stormwater]].<ref name=Eckenfelder>{{Cite book |vauthors=Eckenfelder Jr WW |url=https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/book/10.1002/0471238961 |title=Kirk-Othmer Encyclopedia of Chemical Technology |publisher=[[John Wiley & Sons]] |year=2000 |isbn=978-0-471-48494-3 |doi=10.1002/0471238961.1615121205031105.a01}}</ref> Water pollution may affect either [[surface water]] or [[groundwater pollution|groundwater]]. This form of pollution can lead to many problems. One is the [[environmental degradation|degradation]] of [[aquatic ecosystems]]. Another is spreading [[Waterborne diseases|water-borne diseases]] when people use polluted water for drinking or [[irrigation]].<ref>{{Cite web |date=July 23, 2013 |title=Water Pollution |url=https://www.hsph.harvard.edu/ehep/82-2/ |access-date=September 18, 2021 |website=Environmental Health Education Program |publisher=[[Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health]] |location=Cambridge, MA |archive-date=September 18, 2021 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210918005228/https://www.hsph.harvard.edu/ehep/82-2/ |url-status=live}}</ref> Water pollution also reduces the [[ecosystem service]]s such as [[drinking water]] provided by the [[Water resources|water resource]].

Sources of water pollution are either [[point source]]s or [[non-point source]]s.<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Schaffner |first1=Monika |last2=Bader |first2=Hans-Peter |last3=Scheidegger |first3=Ruth |date=August 15, 2009 |title=Modeling the contribution of point sources and non-point sources to Thachin River water pollution |journal=Science of the Total Environment |volume=407 |issue=17 |pages=4902–4915 |doi=10.1016/j.scitotenv.2009.05.007 |pmid=19501876 |bibcode=2009ScTEn.407.4902S |issn=0048-9697}}</ref> Point sources have one identifiable cause, such as a [[storm drain]], a [[wastewater treatment plant]], or an [[oil spill]]. Non-point sources are more diffuse. An example is [[Surface runoff|agricultural runoff]].<ref name="Moss2008">{{cite journal |vauthors=Moss B |title=Water pollution by agriculture |journal=Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London. Series B, Biological Sciences |volume=363 |issue=1491 |pages=659–666 |date=February 2008 |pmid=17666391 |pmc=2610176 |doi=10.1098/rstb.2007.2176}}</ref> [[Pollution]] is the result of the cumulative effect over time. Pollution may take many forms. One would be toxic substances such as oil, metals, plastics, [[pesticide]]s, [[persistent organic pollutant]]s, and industrial waste products. Another is stressful conditions such as changes of [[pH]], [[Hypoxia (environmental)|hypoxia]] or anoxia, increased temperatures, excessive [[turbidity]], or changes of [[salinity]]). The introduction of [[Pathogen|pathogenic organisms]] is another. Contaminants may include [[organic compound|organic]] and [[inorganic]] substances. A common cause of [[thermal pollution]] is the use of water as a [[coolant]] by [[power plants]] and industrial manufacturers.

The control of water pollution requires appropriate [[infrastructure]] and management plans, as well as legislation. [[Technology]] solutions can include improving [[sanitation]], [[sewage treatment]], [[industrial wastewater treatment]], agricultural [[wastewater treatment]], [[erosion control]], [[sediment control]] and control of [[urban runoff]] (including stormwater management).
{{TOC limit|3}}

== Definition ==
A practical definition of water pollution is: "Water pollution is the addition of substances or energy forms that directly or indirectly alter the nature of the water body in such a manner that negatively affects its legitimate uses."<ref name="Von Sperling"/>{{rp|6}} Water is usually considered polluted when it is contaminated by [[human impact on the environment|anthropogenic]] substances. This means that it either cannot be used for certain purposes, such as for [[drinking water|drinking]], or its ability to support its biotic communities, such as fish, has changed significantly.

== Contaminants ==

===Contaminants with an origin in sewage ===
{{Further|Waterborne diseases#Diseases by type of pathogen|Sewage#Pathogens}}

The following compounds can all reach water bodies via raw [[sewage]] or even treated sewage discharges:
* Various chemical compounds found in personal [[hygiene]] and [[Cosmetics|cosmetic]] products.
* [[Disinfection by-product]]s found in chemically [[disinfection|disinfected]] [[drinking water]] (whilst these chemicals can be a pollutant in the [[Water distribution system|water distribution network]], they are fairly volatile and therefore not usually found in environmental waters).<ref>{{cite journal |vauthors=Alexandrou L, Meehan BJ, Jones OA |title=Regulated and emerging disinfection by-products in recycled waters |journal=[[The Science of the Total Environment]] |volume=637–638 |pages=1607–1616 |date=October 2018 |pmid=29925195 |doi=10.1016/j.scitotenv.2018.04.391 |bibcode=2018ScTEn.637.1607A |s2cid=49355478}}</ref>
* [[Hormone]]s (from [[animal husbandry]] and residue from human [[hormonal contraception]] methods) and synthetic materials such as [[phthalate]]s that mimic hormones in their action. These can have adverse impacts even at very low concentrations on the natural biota and potentially on humans if the water is treated and utilized for drinking water.<ref>{{cite web |title=Environment Agency (archive) – Persistent, bioaccumulative and toxic PBT substances |url=http://www.environment-agency.gov.uk/business/444304/1290036/1290100/1290353/1294402/1311542/?version=1&lang=_e |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20060804195610/http://www.environment-agency.gov.uk/business/444304/1290036/1290100/1290353/1294402/1311542/?version=1&lang=_e |archive-date=August 4, 2006 |access-date=2012-11-14 |publisher=[[Environment Agency]]}}</ref><ref>[http://planetearth.nerc.ac.uk/news/story.aspx?id=297 Natural Environmental Research Council – River sewage pollution found to be disrupting fish hormones] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150427112251/http://planetearth.nerc.ac.uk/news/story.aspx?id=297 |date=April 27, 2015 }}. Planetearth.nerc.ac.uk. Retrieved on 2012-12-19.</ref><ref>{{cite web |title=Endocrine Disruption Found in Fish Exposed to Municipal Wastewater |url=http://toxics.usgs.gov/highlights/wastewater_fish.html |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20111015161223/http://toxics.usgs.gov/highlights/wastewater_fish.html |archive-date=October 15, 2011 |access-date=2012-11-14 |publisher=[[US Geological Survey]] |location=Reston, VA}}</ref>
* [[Insecticide]]s and [[herbicide]]s, often from agricultural runoff.
*[[Pathogen|Pathogens]] like ''[[Hepatovirus A]]'' (HAV may be present in treated wastewater outflows and receiving water bodies but is largely removed during further treatment of drinking water<ref>{{cite journal|vauthors=Takuissu GR, Kenmoe S, Ebogo-Belobo JT, Kengne-Ndé C, Mbaga DS, Bowo-Ngandji A, Ondigui JL, Kenfack-Momo R, Tcatchouang S, Kenfack-Zanguim J, Fogang RL, Menekem EZ, Kame-Ngasse GI, Magoudjou-Pekam JN, Veneri C, Mancini P, Ferraro GB, Iaconelli M, Orlandi L, Del Giudice C, Suffredini E, La Rosa G|display-authors=6|title=Occurrence of Hepatitis A Virus in Water Matrices: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis|year=2023|journal=International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health|volume=20|issue=2|page=1054|article-number=1054|doi=10.3390/ijerph20021054|doi-access=free|pmid=36673812|pmc=9859052}}</ref>)

Inadequately treated wastewater can convey nutrients, pathogens, heterogenous suspended solids and organic fecal matter.<ref name="Von Sperling"/>{{rp|6}}
[[File:Illustration – examples of how water is contaminated (South Asia).jpg|thumb|Poster to teach people in South Asia about human activities leading to the pollution of water sources]]
